1. PLAN OVERVIEW




                   Final Report | 1
                   THIS PAGE IS INTENTIONALLY LEFT BLANK




2 | Final Report
In 2018, the City of Glendale (Glendale) was awarded funding through Maricopa Association of
Government (MAG) to complete the City’s first Active Transportation Plan (ATP). The ATP outlines a
vision to help the City develop a safe and connected active transportation network to encourage
healthy and efficient travel throughout the City and region.

Glendale Overview
Originally settled in the late 1800s and incorporated
in 1910, Glendale has evolved into a thriving, full-
service community with historic charm, beautiful           •
neighborhoods, robust recreational amenities, and
some of the nation’s finest sports and entertainment       •
facilities. Located in the Phoenix metropolitan area’s     •
“West Valley”, Glendale is in one of the fastest
growing areas in the nation, Maricopa County, and is
Arizona’s sixth largest city. Glendale’s Municipal
                                                           •
Planning Area (MPA) covers nearly 92 square miles
and borders six different jurisdictions. Approximately
32 percent of the MPA is located within                    •
unincorporated Maricopa County.

As illustrated in Figure 1.1, the Glendale ATP study area includes the entire Glendale MPA. The
ATP primarily focuses on transportation facilities owned, operated, and/or maintained by Glendale
and how those facilities connect to and intersect with facilities located in other agencies. Other
agencies may include state, local and neighboring jurisdictions, and quasi-government agencies
operating facilities adjacent to or crossing Glendale roadways.

Purpose of the ATP
Glendale initiated the ATP to develop a comprehensive planning guide that outlines a blueprint for
a complete and accessible active transportation network for all people regardless of their age or
ability. The ATP ultimately serves as an action plan that guides decisions and investments for
improving, expanding, and connecting active transportation within Glendale. The actions and
strategies identified in the ATP enable Glendale to strategically overcome active transportation
barriers, support growth, and increase transportation options.

The ATP supersedes the 1990 Bicycle Plan of the City of Glendale. While the 1990 Bicycle Plan
focused only on creating bicycle infrastructure improvements, the ATP explores pedestrian, bicycle,
equestrian, and transit facility needs to accommodate all users regardless of their age or ability.
Since the 1990s, Glendale has worked to enhance and expand its active transportation network;
however, new developments and changing travel behaviors are altering how and why people
travel. Careful and strategic planning is needed to ensure that Glendale’s active transportation
network meets current and future needs.

Why Active Transportation is Important
There are many benefits from active transportation that contribute positively to our quality of life.
Infrastructure investments in trails, sidewalks, traffic-calming, and public transit support active travel
and increase routine physical activity, which improves health, the quality of the environment and
livability of communities. The following are just a few of the benefits of active transportation.

Transportation Choices
Emerging technologies, social trends, and travel behaviors are
altering how people travel. Several studies have shown that
millennials (those born between 1979 and 2001) are driving
less, owning fewer cars, and/or not getting a driver’s license.
The 2017 National Household Travel Survey found that 42
billion miles were walked and biked in the US – imagine
roadway congestion if those miles had been driven instead!

Economic Benefits
Active transportation investments provide numerous economic
benefits including lower transportation costs for individuals;
savings to public agencies and jurisdictions from less wear
and tear on streets; a greater ability for public agencies and
jurisdictions to attract new residents and employers; and a
potential boost in tourism. The Arizona Department of
Transportation’s “Economic Impact Study of Bicycling in


                                                                                                  Final Report | 6
Arizona” reported that $88 million in economic benefits
are generated each year from out-of-state bicycle
tourists visiting Arizona. In Lodi, California streetscape
improvements attracted 60 new stores to the area,
lowering the vacancy rate to 6 percent from 18 percent
and resulting in a 30% increase in sales tax revenues!

Community Livability
Baby boomers, retirees, and millennials are increasingly
moving to locations where they can walk or ride a bike
to access their daily needs. Research conducted by the
Bureau of Transportation Statistics shows that nearly
two-thirds of homebuyers consider the walkability of an
area in their purchase decision. Furthermore, employers
are increasingly looking for locations that offer their
employees walkable and bikeable communities in which
to live, work, and play.

Active Lifestyles and Healthy Community
Public health officials recognize the connection between
mental and physical health and the built environment.
Lack of physical activity is associated with increased risk
of many health problems, particularly obesity, diabetes,
and heart disease. By definition, active transportation
allows people to integrate physical activity into everyday
life by enabling them to walk or bike to their
destinations. Implementing walking and biking facilities
creates access to places where residents can be
physically active and provides more opportunities for
social interaction and community cohesion that have
positive impacts for mental health.

Environmental
By providing facilities for people to walk or bike instead of traveling by vehicle, active transportation
can help address a number of environmental challenges. Research shows that approximately 60
percent of vehicle pollution happens within the first few minutes. Replacing these short vehicle trips
with walking and biking trips can not only reduce car related emissions, but also reduce noise
pollution and congestion. Other environmental benefits include energy savings, less water
pollution, reduced dependency on fossil fuels, and even reduced pressure to develop agricultural
and open spaces.

                                        Final Report | 8
How We Plan
The development of the ATP began in February 2018
and was completed in the following six steps:

   •   Developed the Goals and Objectives. Using input and
       feedback from Glendale staff, the Glendale
       Citizens Active Transportation Advisory Committee
       (CATAC), and the public, goals and objectives
       were established for the ATP.

   •   Understand Current Conditions. Conducted a
       comprehensive inventory of existing land use,
       socioeconomics, safety, roadway, and active
       transportation conditions within the study area.

   •   Identified System Issues, Needs, Gaps and Opportunities.
       Identified gaps, assessed pedestrian and bicycle
       friendliness of the network, and predicted
       potential nonmotorized travel demand through a
       comprehensive system assessment.

   •   Identified and Evaluated Potential Improvement Solutions.
       Identified, evaluated, and prioritized issues,
       needs, and potential facility improvements.

   •   Developed Phased Improvement Plan. Compiled final
       recommendations into a phased implementation
       plan.

The development of the ATP was framed by the League of American Bicyclist’s six “E’s”:
Engineering, Education, Encouragement, Enforcement, Evaluation, and Equity. Community
feedback also guided the overall direction of the ATP. The study included collaboration with a
group of stakeholders and local public agency representatives. To engage the public and
stakeholders, outreach including stakeholder workshops, open houses, online outreach in the form
of a web map was conducted.




                                                                                     Final Report | 9
Who We Plan For
We plan for everyone. The ATP examines facility needs to accommodate all user types and levels of
comfort, including children, commuters, those without access to a vehicle, casual bicyclists,
recreational walkers, and other vulnerable users.

Pedestrians
Walking is the most common form of transportation, as every trip begins and ends by foot. At some
point in the day, everyone is a pedestrian. The range of pedestrian abilities is fairly straightforward
and can be categorized into the following:


                                Walking to complete daily errands, such as shopping, medical
                                appointments, to visit friends/family, etc. Utilitarian walkers need:
                                 • Connected and accessible sidewalks and paths
                                 • Direct connections from residential areas to shopping and
                                   services
                                 • Access to transit centers and bus stops
                                 • Shelter and shade at bus stops



                                Persons that walk or utilize transit as their primary means of
                                transportation to work. Commuter walkers need:
                                  • Direct access routes to major employment centers
                                  • Connected and accessible sidewalks and paths
                                  • Access to transit centers and bus stops
                                  • Shelter and shade at bus stops



                                Those running, jogging, enjoying a leisurely stroll, or walking their
                                dog. Recreation/Fitness walkers need:
                                 • Low stress facilities (i.e., wide sidewalk, sidewalk on local road,
                                   shared use path, etc.)
                                 • Direct connections to residential areas
                                 • Access to parks, open space, and trails



                                Persons utilizing scooters, skateboards, inline skates, etc. to travel
                                faster than by foot. Walkers on small wheels need:
                                 • Connected sidewalks and paths
                                 • Firm and stable surfaces in good condition
                                 • Smooth sidewalk/street transitions
                                 • Gradual cross slopes at driveways



10 | Final Report
Bicyclists
Bicyclist skill level greatly influences expected speeds and
behavior, both in separated bikeways and on shared
roadways. Bicycle infrastructure should accommodate as
many user types as possible, with decisions for separate or
parallel facilities based on providing a comfortable experience
for the greatest number of people. To understand a potential
user’s comfort and willingness to ride a bike on a given
roadway, Roger Geller developed a bicyclist classification
system for the city of Portland, Oregon. This concept was later
defined for the nation by Jennifer Dill and Nathan McNeil at
Portland State University. The classification system categorizes
the general population by their different needs and biking
comfort levels given roadway conditions. The bicycle
classification system categories include the following four
groups:

   •   Strong and Fearless (~6% of the population). Characterizes
       a person who considers bicycling a strong part of their
       identity and will ride anywhere regardless of roadway
       conditions or weather. This type of bicyclist may ride
       faster than other users, prefers direct routes, and will
       typically choose roadway connections over shared use
       paths and trails.
   •   Enthused and Confident (~9% of the population). These
       bicyclists are comfortable riding on roads with motor
       vehicle traffic but prefer to use low traffic stress roads
       or shared use paths.
   •   Interested but Concerned (~60% of the population). This
       group represents the majority of the population and is
       comprised of persons who are interested in bicycling or
       enjoy riding a bike but are afraid to ride on roadways.
       Through experience, education and encouragement,
       people in this category may become “enthused and
       confident” riders.
   •   No Way, No How (~ 25% of the population). Persons in this
       category are not interested in bicycling due to their
       inability to bicycle, perceived safety issues, lack of
       interest, or other constraints. Through education, some
       people in this category may eventually ride a bike.




                                                                    Final Report | 11
ATP Goals, Objectives, and Strategies
Glendale envisions a complete active transportation network that addresses local and regional
issues and needs and provide multimodal transportation options for people of all ages and
abilities. In the spirit of a united and coordinated effort, the ATP used goals, objectives, and
strategies developed by Glendale that guides all long-term transportation planning by the City.
    •    A Goal is what the City of Glendale wants to achieve over the long term.
    •    An Objective is a specific, measurable statement that marks progress towards the goal.
    •    A Strategy is a continual action that guides decision-making to achieve the objectives and
         goals of the Plan.
Table 1.1 outlines the City's overall transportation goals. Tables 1.2-1.3 outline active
transportation specific objectives and strategies developed by the City.

          movement of goods consistent with the City of Glendale’s vision and values.
          Provide transportation infrastructure that promotes economic development initiatives within the
    2     undeveloped Municipal Planning Area and supports infill development opportunities within
          “established” Glendale.
          Comprehensively plan for all locally significant modes of transportation and work towards their
    3     interconnection. Coordinate with other jurisdictions and agencies that either influence or are
          affected by Glendale’s transportation planning efforts.
          Develop a balanced land‐use and transportation system that reflects the overall need to minimize
    4     automobile travel and maximizes the opportunities for transportation alternatives such as public

About Glendale
Situated in the heart of the Sonoran Desert in south-central Arizona, Glendale’s landscape varies
between low desert valleys and developed urbanized areas. Over 94 percent of the MPA is privately
owned, with the rest of the MPA being comprised of military, Arizona State Trust Land, and other
land holders. Numerous water bodies transverse Glendale, including the New River, Agua Fria
River, Skunk Creek, Arizona Canal, Grand Canal, and the Airline Canal.

General Character Areas
Glendale’s development patterns and community characteristics vary greatly. Broadly speaking,
Glendale can be generally divided into four distinctive areas (see Figure 2.1) characterized by their
development patterns and existing active transportation networks.

 Northern Developed Area
 General Location: MPA area north of Peoria Avenue
 General Characteristics: Mix of residential developments and key
 commercial corridors. Blend of young families, middle aged
 persons, and retirees. Large “snowbird” population.
 Key Activity Centers: Arrowhead Towne Center, Thunderbird
 Conservation Area, Thunderbird Medical Complex, and
 Midwestern University.                                                   Arrowhead Towne Center

 Central Core
 General Location: MPA area south of Peoria Avenue and east of
 75th Avenue
 General Characteristics: Older, established downtown Glendale
 with a mixture of single- and multi-family residential areas and
 large industrial areas.
 Key Activity Centers: Downtown Glendale, Glendale Community
 College                                                                   Downtown Glendale

 Loop 101 Area
 General Location: MPA area west of 75th Avenue to the Agua
 Fria River
 General Characteristics: Developing area with a blend of master
 planned residential areas, major entertainment and
 commercial districts, and vast open agricultural lands.
 Key Activity Centers: State Farm Stadium, Westgate Entertainment
 District                                                         Westgate Entertainment District

 Western Planning Area
 General Location: MPA area west of the Agua Fria River
 Community Character: Mixture of large master-planned residential
 communities, large-lot single family properties, developed
 land surrounding Luke Air Force Base, and agriculture lands.
 Key Activity Centers: Luke Air Force Base, Wildlife World Zoo
                                                                          Luke Air Force Base

Population Overview
Population characteristics such as age, socioeconomic status, disability status, etc., contribute to an
understanding of the needs of potential active transportation users. To determine current
population characteristics, socioeconomic data was compiled from the US Census Bureau’s 2010
Census and 2012-2016 American Community Survey (ACS).

Table 2.1 summarizes the study area’s current population information and Figure 2.2 illustrates
areas with the highest concentration of population. The highest concentrations of population occur
along Bethany Home Road, Glendale Avenue, 59th Avenue, and 43rd Avenue in Glendale’s Central
Core area. These areas are largely zoned as high density residential, which includes a mix of
apartments, condominiums, and single-family dwelling units.

                                                                                                                Final Report | 20
Land Use and Major Destinations
Land use and active transportation are directly connected. Dense and mixed-use areas with major
destinations and services typically have more convenient biking and walking distances than single-
use suburban and rural development areas. Figure 2.3 illustrates major active transportation
generators in the Glendale MPA, including:
   •   Major Employers. Glendale is a growing employment market in the Phoenix metropolitan
       area’s West Valley and home to several major employers. Major employment sectors
       include healthcare, entertainment, retail, and military. Luke Air Force Base is the largest
       employer in the study area, employing over 3,400 persons. Banner Health Thunderbird
       Medical Center is the second largest employer, with over 2,800 employees.
   •   K-12 Schools. In total, there are over 54 K-12 schools in the Glendale MPA with over 50,000
       students. The largest schools in the study area include Copper Canyon High School,
       Mountain Ridge High School, and Ironwood High School. Providing active transportation
       facilities is critical since many children are likely to have short commutes that could be
       converted to walking or biking trips.
   •   Higher Education. Glendale has several private and public higher education, including the
       Glendale Community College, Midwestern University, and the Arizona Christian University.
       These educational facilities have a high demand for active transportation facilities. Glendale
       Community College, alone, has over 31,000 enrolled students that frequently walk, bike,
       and use transit to attend school.
   •   Health Care Facilities. There are several major medical/healthcare facilities located in the study
       area. Major healthcare facilities include Banner Health Systems, Abrazo Arrowhead
       Hospital, Humana Health Care, Midwestern University, and St. Joseph’s Westgate.
   •   Entertainment Districts. Glendale is home to some of the Nation’s finest sports and
       entertainment areas. Along the Loop 101, the Westgate Entertainment District hosts major
       sport venues, shopping outlets, and dining facilities. In the northern portion of the MPA,
       there are numerous golf courses and the Arrowhead Towne Center shopping area.
   •   Commercial Centers. Major retail centers are located near most major subdivisions and along
       major roadways. Grocery stores or big-box retailers are typically the anchor store for these
       centers. Commercial centers have the potential to generate significant pedestrian and
       bicycle traffic.
   •   Retirement Communities. As a person ages, their reliance on alternative transportation
       increases. Active transportation can provide elderly persons a way of accessing jobs,
       services, and goods, as well as facilitate healthy aging through physical activity.

Major Residential Areas
Concentrations of housing developments may be indicative of a demand for active transportation
facilities. Providing direct and convenient active transportation connections between major
residential communities and key activity centers creates opportunities to benefit a large number of
residents. In the Glendale MPA, over 35 percent of the area is zoned as single-family or multifamily
residential. Large residential developments in the far Western Planning Area, such as Cortessa and
White Tank Foothills, create obstacles to active transportation as their locations are far removed
from existing facilities.



                                                                                           Final Report | 21
22 | Final Report
Final Report | 23
24 | Final Report
Final Report | 25
Commute Travel Behavior
Knowing where people walk or bike, and for what purposes, informs Glendale how to effectively
implement projects and programs that better serve residents. Glendale is a car-oriented
community. ACS data shows that in 2016, 2.1% of workers commuted to work daily by walking or
biking, while 2.7% used public transit. Although these statistics are important to the overall
discussion, they only include a portion of active transportation commuters and fail to measure non-
commute activities such as trips to stores, to schools, or for recreation.




         Homearea.com showed that Glendale has the highest commute times in the Phoenix
           metropolitan area, increasing by 6.4 percent from 26.5 in 2015 to 28.2 in 2018!
Trip Reduction Program
The Maricopa Regional Travel Reduction Program (TRP), which surveys employers and students to
determine commuting habits, provides a more accurate assessment of the total number of
employees/students traveling by alternative modes. According to the 2015 TRP survey, commuters
in Maricopa County walking and biking account for nearly 5 percent of all commuters that use
alternative modes. Of students that use alternative modes, over 8.6 percent walk, which is an
increase of 0.9 percent since the 2014 survey. Results of the TRP survey show employees commute
15.0 miles to work on average, while students travel an average of 7.1 miles to school. Overall,
27.6% of all TRP participants drive less than 5 miles to work/school. These 5-mile trips have the
potential to be converted to walking and biking trips through a connected, efficient active
transportation network.

Socioeconomic Equity Analysis
A socioeconomic equity analysis model was developed for
the ATP to identify areas with a high percentage of
population groups that traditionally rely on active
transportation as their primary means of transportation.
Providing active transportation connections to areas with a
high concentration of disadvantaged population groups
may help alleviate wider social issues such as access to
jobs, healthy food, education, and healthcare.
The socioeconomic equity analysis model identified levels
of socioeconomic need using an index of the following
indicators:

   •   Age – children and elderly populations
   •   Ethnicity – minority populations
   •   Disabled Populations – persons that have cognitive, visual and physical disabilities
   •   Low-Income – households that are financially less likely to own a vehicle
   •   Vehicle Ownership – households with limited or no access to a vehicle
Results of this model are displayed in Figure 2.4. Areas with the highest percentage of population
groups that traditionally rely more on walking, bicycling, or transit as their primary form of
transportation are depicted as having the higher socioeconomic need. As stated by the goals of the
ATP, Glendale expressed its commitment to providing active transportation options to vulnerable
users and traditionally underserved communities. As the ATP is implemented, additional social
equity impacts, such as burden of construction on disadvantaged population groups, should be
considered beyond those included in the prioritization process

30 | Final Report
Public Health Equity Analysis
As part of the ATP process, a health equity model was
developed to identify areas with disproportionately
high rates of chronic diseases that may be prevented
or controlled through physical activity. Studies show
that physical inactivity is linked to higher rates of
heart disease and stroke. Walking and biking are
basic forms of physical activity and can potentially
prevent or control chronic diseases such as heart
disease, stroke, diabetes, and high cholesterol and
help to improve mood and lower stress levels.
According to the Maricopa County Community
Health Improvement Plan, cardiovascular disease is
the second leading cause of death in Maricopa
County. Furthermore, approximately 29 percent of Glendale residents are obese. Providing
pedestrian and bicycle facilities that are accessible and convenient may encourage activity limited,
overweight, and obese population groups to incorporate physical activity into their lifestyle.

The health equity analysis model identified the rate of the following health indicators:

   •   Stroke
   •   Coronary Heart Disease (CHD)
   •   Diabetes
   •   Asthma
   •   High Cholesterol
   •   No Leisure-time Physical Activity
   •   Obesity
Results of the model are displayed in Figure 2.5. Utilizing the model, identifying the presence and
completeness of active transportation facilities in areas with the highest rates of chronic health
diseases can help Glendale align with its goals of fostering an active-living culture. By providing
convenient active transportation facilities, people may be more inclined to walk or bike, and in turn
help control chronic diseases while also improving mood and lowering stress levels. To aid the City
in determining potential improvements that may help reduce public health issues, the project
prioritization process, developed for the ATP, included health equity model results.

     3. OUR CURRENT
TRANSPORTATION NETWORK



                   Final Report | 35
This chapter presents a synopsis of key roadway and active transportation conditions within the
Glendale MPA. The purpose of this chapter is to create an overall understanding of the City as it is
now, what it can become, and to develop a data-driven foundation upon which future
recommendations can be built.

Existing Roadway Network
The following section summarizes typical characteristics of existing roadway conditions and
characteristics in the Glendale MPA.

Major Travel Corridors
Travel corridors connect communities, land uses, employment centers, and link people to goods
and services. Traditionally, roadways are grouped into a hierarchical classification, which helps
identify the roadway’s function, design, speed limits, access control, and adjacent land use
development. Understanding roadway classification is imperative when planning an active
transportation network. Vehicle volumes, number of lanes, lane width, road condition, and speed
limits impact a pedestrian and bicyclist’s level of comfort. As illustrated in Figure 3.1, within the
Glendale MPA there is a mixture of roadways, including:

 Freeway/Expressway
 Controlled access roadway that provide regional
 connections. Typically has high speeds and high traffic
 volumes that make it unfavorable for pedestrian and bicycle
 usage.
 Parkway
 Enhanced arterial that eliminates left-turn movements at
 intersections. Connects regional destinations and
 communities.
 Arterial
 Major roadway with multiple travel lanes and higher traffic
 volumes and speeds. Typically, is lined with dense
 commercial and retail land uses and major destinations.
 Connects regional destinations and communities. Bike lanes
 may be applicable for experienced bicyclists only.
 Collector
 Larger corridor that has moderate traffic volumes and speeds.
 Distributes traffic from local roads and neighborhoods to
 arterials. With proper facilities, provides low stress facility for
 pedestrian and bicycle usage.

 Local
 Minor roadway with lower traffic volumes and speeds.
 Provides direct access within a neighborhood. Provides a low-
 stress facility for all users to walk and bike.




36 | Final Report
Due to high traffic volumes, arterials traditionally have numerous businesses, commercial services,
transit stops, and other major destinations that attract pedestrians and bicyclists and, in turn,
potentially create conflicts with motorists, particularly at intersections. Greater separation between
the vehicle lanes and active transportation infrastructure is desirable along arterials. On lower
classification roads such as collectors and local streets, bicyclists feel comfortable sharing lanes (on
roads with less traffic) and pedestrians feel safer because of the lower traffic volumes and more
frequent crossing opportunities.

Roadway Speed Limits
Speed is a significant factor in determining the potential
use of pedestrian and bicycle facilities. The National
Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA) reports
that the occurrence of pedestrian crashes and risk of
severe injury or death are both strongly associated with
the travel speed of the motor vehicle at the time of the
crash. AAA Foundation for Traffic Safety’s 2011 Impact
Speed and a Pedestrian’s Risk of Severe Injury or Death
report found that at 15 MPH, pedestrians had a 95
percent survival rate, compared to 40 percent at 45
MPH. Risk of injury also increases significantly with age.
For example, a 70-year old pedestrian struck by a
vehicle traveling at 25 MPH has a similar risk of severe injury or death as a 30-year-old struck at
35 MPH. Figure 3.2 illustrates posted speed limits within the Glendale MPA.

Vehicle Volumes
Very similar to speed characteristics, traffic volume is a significant factor in determining the use
potential of a pedestrian or bicycle facility. Corridors with high traffic volumes and high vehicle
speeds may reduce a bicyclist or pedestrians level of comfort, discouraging them from using the
corridor. Figure 3.3 illustrates current traffic volumes.

Number of Lanes, Widths, and Right-of-Way
Travel lane characteristics, in conjunction with available right-of-way, play a key role in the
expansion potential of pedestrian and bicycle facilities. Number of lanes and lane widths are
integral in determining the stress level for bicyclists. Opportunities to increase the active
transportation network within a roadway’s existing right-of-way or roadway width include adding a
paved shoulder or bike lane, narrowing a wide lane and installing a bike lane, constructing an off-
set shared use path within a wide right-of-way, or constructing a sidewalk along a narrow roadway.
In urban areas, however, right-of-way often is limited, making pedestrian and bicycle improvements
challenging. Typically, arterial roadways have a lane width of 12 FT. Restriping an urban arterial
road to an 11FT travel lane can provide opportunities to install sidewalks, wider bike lanes, wider
curb lanes to accommodate bikes, or to provide roadside landscaped buffers. Figure 3.4 illustrates
current number of travel lanes.

Driver Action
Over 25 percent of pedestrian-related crashes and
nearly 44 percent of bicycle-related crashes occurred                          Right-Hook
when a motor vehicle was turning right. This
maneuver, often called a “right hook,” takes place
when a driver cuts off a bicyclist or pedestrian by
turning right across their path onto a road or a
driveway. In addition, over 17 percent of pedestrian-     Left-Hook
related crashes and 15 percent of bicycle-related
crashes occurred when motorists made a left turn.
Left turn crashes are commonly due to a motorist not seeing a pedestrian or bicyclist when turning
left onto a road or intersection

Existing Pedestrian Facilities and Conditions
Sidewalks provide a place for people to walk for commuting or recreational purposes; they are
especially important for providing independence to the mobility impaired or persons without access
to a vehicle. In addition to sidewalks, pedestrian facilities such as crossings, curb ramps, curb
extensions, traffic calming features, and other improvements help create a more comfortable
walking environment. In order to understand the walking environment in Glendale, an extensive
mapping exercise and inventory of sidewalks and curb ramps was conducted. Glendale’s 2015
Engineering Design and Construction Standards provides guidelines for pedestrian facilities,
including:

    •    6 FT (minimum) sidewalks for all arterial streets, while sidewalks along collector and local
         roadways minimum of 5 FT wide.
    •    Detectable warnings are required whenever the walking surface is not separated from the
         roadway by curbs, railings, or other approved elements
Figure 3.7 illustrates the location and width of pedestrian sidewalks. Sporadic corridor and business
development has caused gaps in Glendale’s existing pedestrian network, which creates barriers to
pedestrian travel.

Sidewalk Conditions
Sidewalks are the backbone of any pedestrian network; their condition affects all pedestrians,
particularly individuals with disabilities. Uneven surfaces, obstructions, or poor sidewalk conditions
create deterrents or barriers in the pedestrian network.

Intersection Traffic
To complete the pedestrian network, sidewalks are supported by a collection of facilities to create a
more visible, navigable, and enjoyable walking experience. Facilities include signage, crosswalks,
curb ramps, median refuge islands, overpasses, and signalized pedestrian crossings. At signalized
intersections, it is essential that are signals phased and timed to allow pedestrians of all abilities to
cross the roadway, including those who are typically slower (children, senior citizens, people with
limited mobility). At the same time, signal delay must be minimized to reduce the amount of illegal
and unsafe crossing that occurs when pedestrians get impatient waiting for the signal to change.

To provide pedestrian’s with mid-block crossing locations, Glendale currently has pedestrian
HAWK signals at the following locations (including the date signals went live):
    •    61st Avenue and Bethany Home Road (5/21/2011)
    •    63rd Avenue and Beardsley Road (8/1/2012)
    •    65th Avenue and Bethany Home Road (9/10/2015)
    •    65th Avenue and Glendale Avenue (1/16/2018)

Existing Bicycle Facilities and Conditions
Bicycling is an essential component of any transportation system that benefits numerous areas
including health, economics, environment, and equity. Since the 1990 Bicycle Plan, Glendale has
made progress in providing bicycle facilities along its maintained roadways.

Location and Types of Bicycle Facilities
In total, there are 42.5 centerline miles of bike lanes and routes, 7.9 miles of paved shoulders, and
30.6 miles of shoulder stripe with parking within the Glendale MPA. Of the roadways with bike
facilities, 40% have on-street parking. In addition, there are 72.4 miles of shared use paths. As
illustrated in Figure 3.8, bicycle facilities are primarily located on collector streets. Shared use paths
are largely located around master planned communities, which may be attributed to developers
constructing the paths during development. Currently the City does not have specific design
guidelines for bicycle facilities. For the purposes of the active transportation inventory, typical
bicycle facilities available in the Glendale MPA include:
    • Bike Lane – striped lane (4 FT or wider) may include pavement marking and a designated
         buffer space separating the bike lane from motor vehicle travel lanes. Facilities may have
         curb and gutter present or a flush shoulder.
    • Bike Route – signed shared roadway that provides shared use for bicyclist and vehicles.
    • Paved Shoulder – wide shoulder adjacent to the roadway that provides a functional space
         for bicyclists to travel. Typically, does not have curb and gutter.
    • Shoulder Stripe with Parking – 5 to 6 FT wide pavement striping where parking is not
         prohibited.
    • Shared Use Path - bidirectional pathway that is physically separated from motorized
         vehicular traffic by an open space or barrier. Shared use paths may be used by pedestrians,
         bicyclists, skaters, wheelchair users, joggers and other nonmotorized users.

Regional Bikeway Networks
Regional bicycle networks help to resolve complex, interrelated issues concerning traffic congestion,
air quality, public health, and livability. Glendale’s bicycle network is supported by a larger,
regionwide network of bicycle facilities that has been developed and is maintained by the numerous
municipalities and agencies in the region. Collectively, this regional bicycle network consists of a
combination of bike lanes, bike routes, paved shoulders, and shared use paths. While the network
is vast and far-reaching, Glendale has a tremendous opportunity to collaborate with neighboring
jurisdictions to expand the region’s bicycle network.

Off Street Paths and Trails
When bicycle and pedestrian facilities are connected to recreational areas they act as an extension
of the transportation system. Connecting parks and other recreational facilities via bicycle and
pedestrian facilities is a way to make parks more accessible and provide a safe and convenient
means for residents to explore the recreational system. Off-street paths and trails are open to
bikers, walkers, hikers, runners, and often equestrians. Figure 3.8 illustrates the locations of major
local and regional trails.

Existing Transit Services
Understanding the location of transit routes and stops is critical when developing a walking and
biking network, because almost every transit trip begins or ends with walking and/or biking. Often
people who could potentially utilize transit choose to drive because no transit stops are conveniently
located near their starting points or final destinations. Placing walking and biking facilities along
“first and last mile” paths expands a person’s transportation choices by making transit more
accessible. Integrating active transportation networks and transit helps to create a balanced and
efficient multimodal transportation network that makes transportation affordable, convenient, and
flexible for all users regardless of their age, ability, or socioeconomic status.

In the study area, the City of Glendale, Valley Metro, and the City of Phoenix provide public
transportation services, including:
    •    Glendale Urban Shuttle (GUS) – free,
         local neighborhood circulator system
         that connect residential areas to major
         destinations and the regional transit
         network. All GUS vehicles are
         wheelchair accessible and include
         bicycle racks.
    •    Fixed-Route – 13 fixed-routes operate
         in Glendale, primarily along arterial
         roads. Service hours and frequency
         vary by route, as well as the condition
         of bus stops.                                                Picture of the Glendale Urban Shuttle (GUS) free, local neighborhood
                                                                                                                           circulator shuttle
    •    Express Bus – two express bus routes
         provide peak hour service to and from
         downtown Phoenix.
Existing transit service routes within the study area are shown in Figure 3.9.

Bus Stops
Bus stops provide a safe boarding location for passengers while each route provides options for
Glendale residents to access nearby and regional destinations. Typically bus stops are located at
one‐quarter mile intervals for convenience. In addition to Valley Metro routes, Glendale operates
three circulator services called Glendale Urban Shuttle (GUS). GUS 1 is a downtown circulator that
provides daily services in a 30-minute loop. GUS 2 and 3 are larger community circulator routes
that provide service Monday through Friday. More information on GUS can be found at
https://www.glendaleaz.com/transit/GUS.cfm.

In 2017, Valley Metro conducted a bus stop inventory to determine what types of facilities are
present at each stop. Data developed by Valley Metro was verified and updated to reflect existing
conditions in Glendale. Within the study area, there are 539 bus stops with varying amenities.
Typical facilities found within the study area include:
    • Bus Shelters – 172 bus stops are sheltered
    • Bicycle Racks – 10 bus stops are equipped with bicycle racks
   • Bicycle Lockers – Glendale Ave/99th Ave Park-and-Ride has four bike lockers
   • Benches – 224 bus stops have benches
   • Sidewalk Connectivity – 26 bus stops do not have sidewalk connections
Figure 3.10 illustrates the results of the bus stop inventory.

Active Transportation Demand Analysis
The active transportation demand model estimates
the level of potential walking and biking trips
based on where people live, work, play, access
transit, go to school, and other factors known to
influence travel behavior. Often referred to as
“Latent Demand”, the model looks at potential
trip generators (i.e., where people begin or end
their trips such as home or work), as well as trip
attractors (i.e., school, library, places to shop,
etc.).

The results of the Active Transportation Demand
Model, shown in Figure 3.11, illustrate a high
demand for walking and biking. Glendale areas
with the highest latent demand include:

   •   Areas along Bell Road, Greenway Road,
       and Union Hills Drive in the Northern
       Developed area
   •   Downtown Glendale
   •   Areas along Olive Avenue, Northern
       Avenue, and Bethany Home Road in the
       Downtown Core area
   •   Areas along Bethany Home Road
   •   Residential areas near the Luke Air Force
       Base
Demand results provide important information on
where active transportation investments are most
needed. It is important to note though, that
distance is an important predictor for where
active transportation investments may be the most
impactful. Even if an appealing and comfortable
path is available, the average person is unlikely
to travel more than five miles by bike or a mile on
foot to commute to work or school, grocery
shopping, etc.




                                                      Final Report | 67
68 | Final Report
Final Report | 69
Pedestrian Level of Comfort
While each person experiences a different level of comfort when
walking, there are basic roadway characteristics that affect the
user experience. Nationally, there is no standard methodology to
quantify a pedestrian’s level of comfort; however, a pedestrian’s
comfort is primarily affected by factors such as:
    •    Sidewalks: existing/non-existing, buffer, condition
    •    Roadways: width, speed limit, vehicle volumes
Generally, wider pedestrian spaces, lower speed limits, and the
presence of a buffer correlate to a higher comfort level. An
incomplete sidewalk network, higher speeds, and a greater
number of lanes correlate to a lower comfort level. Bicycle lanes
or on-street parking act as buffers between pedestrians and
vehicle traffic, increasing comfort. Examples of sidewalks with
varying comfort levels are shown on the right.
Sidewalks in poor condition also lower a pedestrian’s level of
comfort, as they pose tripping hazards and make pushing a
stroller, skateboarding, or using an assistive mobility device more
difficult. To identify the potential comfort of Glendale’s pedestrian
network, a complete inventory of sidewalk locations, condition,
width, and presence of curb ramps was conducted. The inventory
was the basis for developing pedestrian network improvements.




70 | Final Report
Bicycle Level of Traffic Stress (LTS)
Bicycle Level of Traffic Stress (LTS) quantifies the perceived sense of comfort associated with biking
along a given roadway. Originally developed by Alex Sorton and the Northwestern University Traffic
Institute, LTS has become the industry standard for assessing the comfort and connectivity of bicycle
networks. For the Glendale ATP, the following roadway characteristics were used to predict the
stress experience (or comfort level) of bicyclists:
   •   Posted speed limit
   •   Number of lanes
   •   Annual average daily traffic volumes (AADT)
   •   Presence (and width) of a bicycle facility
   •   Presence (and width) of on-street parking
Each roadway was assigned an LTS score between 1 (low stress) and 5 (high stress). The most
desirable bicycling score, LTS 1, is assigned to roads that would be suitable for most children to
ride. Roadways that are scored LTS 4 or 5 are only suitable for “strong and fearless” bicyclists who
tolerate roadways with high vehicle speeds and volumes.

                                                                                                     Level of Stress 4
                                               Level of Stress 2                                     • 1% of Glendale roads
                                               • 1% of Glendale                                      • More stressful, bicycle
 Total Miles by Level of Traffic Stress          roads                                                 facility present
                                               • Low stress, suitable                                • Suitable for traffic-
                                                 for majority of the                                   tolerant cyclist
                                                 population




                Level of Stress 1                                       Level of Stress 3
               • 76% of Glendale roads                                  • 1% of Glendale roads       Level of Stress 5
               • Low stress, requires little                            • Moderate stress            • 20% of Glendale
                 attention                                                                             roads
                                                                        • Suitable for adults with
               • Suitable for all ages and                                confidence on a bicycle    • Most stressful, with
                 abilities, including                                                                  no bicycle facility
                 children                                                                              present


As shown in Figure 3.12, the majority of the roadway network within the Glendale MPA is LTS 1
(shown in light blue). These LTS 1 roadways are primarily local, internal neighborhood roads and
not necessarily a part of the regional bicycle network. These low-stress facilities are primarily
smaller, local roadways within residential areas. In total, nearly 79 percent of roadways within the
study area are classified as low to moderate stress (LTS 1 – LTS 3). Moderate- to high-stress
roadways are primarily arterials that provide regional connections. It is important to note that this
analysis represents existing stress level conditions; therefore, the addition or widening of bicycle
facilities may improve a user’s perceived level of comfort and in turn improve a roadway’s LTS
score. The study area has a large network of roadways that can be utilized to develop a complete
and connected bicycle network to encourage “No Way, Now How” and “Interested but
Concerned” riders to get on their bikes.

improvements were needed. To garner input from a wide cross-section of the public, multiple
outreach methods were utilized. Comments received supported the fact that people who live and
work in Glendale care about safety and convenience when traveling. Priorities centered on closing
gaps in the local and regional network, educating motorists on pedestrian and bicycle safety,
improving crossings, and preserving and maintaining existing facilities.

                    Cross Section                        Number of “Likes”   Percentage of Total
 Shared lanes (parking on both sides)                            1                   2%

The large majority of respondents preferred either buffered bike
lanes (49 percent) or bike lanes with parking on one side (33
percent). Safety was the most common concern – respondents felt
the buffer added much needed space between vehicles and
bicycles. When parking was important to the respondent, most
were willing to give up parking on one side of the street to
accommodate a buffer for the bike lane adjacent to the parking
lane. There was some concern that on-street parking on local and
collector streets is unsafe for bicyclists, particularly for young
children. Only one respondent preferred a cross section with no
dedicated bicycle lanes.

The project team also asked participants to identify any areas
throughout the City where more bicycle facilities were needed. Two
locations were identified:
    •    59th Avenue between Loop 101 and Deer Valley Road
    •    The intersection of Bethany Home Road and 107th Avenue

Public/Stakeholder Open House
Public/Stakeholder Open House #2
The purpose of the first phase of stakeholder/public outreach
was to seek input from the public regarding the existing and
future deficiencies and needs. To provide ample
opportunities for residents to participate, two separate
meetings at the Glendale Public Library from 11:00 am to
1:00 pm and 5:00 pm to 7:00 pm. The meetings were
advertised to City residents through social media, radio
spots, fliers, email, and NextDoor. Direct email invitations
were also sent to representatives from local community
groups, bike shops, colleges, and area stakeholders.

The meetings were held in open house style, with attendees
invited to casually mingle with the study team. A series of
boards were provided that outlined identified conditions,
issues, and concerns in the City. Attendees were asked to
identify additional issues, needs, and improvement ideas
utilizing stickers or in writing. Comment forms were
also distributed to gain additional input from
attendees. A Spanish translator was also in attendance
to accommodate Spanish speaking participants. In
total, there were 24 members of the community in
attendance, not including study team members. Key
comments received during the meetings included:
     •    Lack of connectivity was repeatedly mentioned
          as a major impedance.
     •    Multiple residents cited concerns regarding
          safe access to school for children using various
          modes.
     •    Bicyclists and pedestrians have a difficult time
          crossing Grand Avenue.
     •    Narrow bike lanes on arterial streets make
          bicycling dangerous.
     •    Paved shoulders that allow for automobile
          parking are a nuisance for bicyclists.
     •    Connections between multi-use paths and
          streets need to be enhanced.
     •    Many neighborhoods lack ADA-compliant
          sidewalks and driveways.
     •    Bicycle facilities abruptly end at river crossings.
     •    Rideshare services (scooters) are beginning to be a nuisance, as the users are riding on the
          sidewalks and parking on private property.

       situations.

       Loop 101 pedestrian bridge at 63rd Avenue.

Q. 6: Why do you walk or bike?
Understanding why users walk or bike informs decisions on future facilities. Key findings show:
    •    64 percent of respondents shared that they walk or bike for fun, recreation, or exercise.
    •    MCPHD survey respondents ride their bikes to work or school or to run errands (48 percent
         to go to work or school; 34 percent to run errands) more than online survey respondents
         (21 percent to go to work or school; 19 percent to run errands).
    •    Lack of car ownership may account for this difference as a much higher percentage of
         MCPHD respondents (50 percent) do not own a vehicle compared to the online respondents
         (3 percent).

most commonly reported barriers. Other barriers cited included: lack of driver awareness and
education, weather/heat, and gaps in the network.

92 | Final Report
5. ACTIVE TRANSPORTATION
    RECOMMENDATIONS




                     Final Report | 93
The need and recommendations identified in the Glendale ATP address gaps in the active
transportation network that create barriers and may discourage people from choosing to walk, ride
a bike, or access transit.

Identification of Needs
Identification of active transportation needs and
potential projects is the first step in developing the
future active transportation network. To begin, a
comprehensive listing of all locations that may
benefit from pedestrian and/or bicycle facilities
improvements was developed. Developing a list of
potential improvements included a multi-step
process involving complex data analysis and
ongoing dialogue with Glendale staff, stakeholders,
and the public. These improvements included:

    •    Linear improvement locations that lack
         pedestrian and/or bicycle facilities, creating
         a gap in the local and regional system;
    •    Spot improvement locations, including
         intersections lacking sidewalks or
         bicycle facilities, inadequate railroad
         crossings, and vertical gaps;
    •    First and last-mile connections to
         transit for people walking and biking;
    •    Potential freeway, canal, railroad or river
         crossing locations;
    •    Locations where a low-stress bicycle network
         can be developed;
    •    Locations where strategic improvements can
         aid in expanding the regional active
         transportation network, such as additional
         access to regional parks and trails, or new
         routes to create new long-distance cycling
         routes; and
    •    Locations identified by the public, local
         agencies, and Glendale staff that would
         benefit from improved pedestrian and/or
         bicycle facilities.
Gaps in the network may be due to many factors, including but not limited to; inconsistent corridor
development, physical constraints, and right-of-way issues. Filling-in these gaps has the potential to
link thousands of people to jobs and provide choices for convenient travel by foot or bicycle. Figure
5.1. illustrates gaps and needs identified in the Glendale MPA.




94 | Final Report

Final Report | 97
Prioritization of Improvement Needs
Once a complete set of infrastructure improvement needs were identified, a prioritization tool was
developed to quantify the magnitude of each project’s contribution to the overall vision and goals
of the ATP. The prioritization tool is a flexible approach, intended to provide clear direction for
proactively seeking project funds and completing design and engineering of the most critical
projects, while still allowing for opportunistic implementation of the entire network.

Project Prioritization Criteria
Five key prioritization categories were identified and weights were assigned based on their overall
contribution to the goals of the ATP Key prioritization criteria included:
    • Safety: project addresses a location that has a history of pedestrian- or bicycle-related
       crashes and provides a less stressful facility to users of all ages and abilities;
    •     Access and Connectivity: project fills a gap in the system and improves pedestrian and
          bicycle access to key destinations, including critical facilities and transit;
    •     Demand: project likely addresses unmet nonmotorized travel demand or has the potential to
          increase active transportation trips;
    •     Equity: project serves an underserved area with population groups that traditionally rely on
          nonmotorized transportation; and
    •     Feasibility: project has few physical constraints and is a modest investment.
The results of the prioritization process are not intended to preclude projects from receiving funding
or from being incorporated into a new development or other roadway project. All recommended
improvement projects have merit and should be implemented as timely and cost-effective
opportunities arise.

            Safety                    Connectivity                   Demand                          Equity                   Feasibility

                                                                                            area with population

                                                                                                                             Final Report | 98
Project Prioritization Criteria Scoring
For each of the criteria presented in Figure 5.2, measurable factors (referred to as sub criteria)
were first identified and an objective scoring system was created that rates each project on a scale
of 0 to 100. Points were allocated to each project based on GIS data analysis, creating a
transparent, data driven process that identifies the projects that are most consistent with the goals
of this plan.
Table 5.1 outlines the project prioritization sub criteria scoring utilized. Ultimately, the sub criteria
scores for each project are combined to generate an overall project score. A high priority project (a
project with a higher overall score) provides the greatest benefit for the criteria identified. Glendale
staff should regularly revisit the project list and reapply the prioritization methodology to help
schedule short-term improvements.

104 | Final Report
Pedestrian Network Improvement Recommendations
The approach to developing the pedestrian network intends to concentrate resources in areas
where improvements are most needed and where people are most likely to walk. Proposed
pedestrian improvements aim to close sidewalk gaps and provide a safe and comfortable
experience for users of all ages and abilities. Combined with Glendale’s extensive existing
pedestrian network, the identified pedestrian needs create a more robust, connected, and
comfortable walking network throughout the study area. This section describes the recommended
pedestrian network improvements. Network improvements are categorized as follows:
    •     Sidewalk Repairs: sidewalks identified as being in poor condition at the time of the
          inventory.
    •     Sidewalk Infill Improvements: short-trip pedestrian facility improvements at specific locations
          where gaps occur in the overall pedestrian network.
    •     Intersection Improvements: improve pedestrian safety and comfort when crossing the road
          as well as minimize any potential conflicts with vehicles.
    •     Transit Connections: addresses first/last mile issues by providing needed pedestrian
          connections to and from the public transportation network.
    •     Accessibility for People with Disabilities: sidewalk and other pedestrian facility improvements
          to accommodate all persons with cognitive, visual, or physical disabilities.
Appendix A provides a full listing of improvement recommendations, their priority scoring, and a
planning level cost estimate.

Sidewalk Repair
Sidewalk repair projects are proposed for sidewalks identified as
being in poor condition at the time of inventory. Sidewalk issues
may include obstructions, large cracks, uneven surfaces, and
damaged segments. Sidewalks in need of repair can limit
pedestrian access and pose safety concerns for pedestrians.
Figure 5.2 illustrates the location of sidewalks identified as in
poor condition and Table 5.2 provides examples of repair needs.

Intersection Improvements
Intersections are potential conflict points between pedestrians and vehicles and thus require design
treatments that set clear expectations for all roadway users. Every intersection in Glendale should
be designed for pedestrian safety and comfort, with pedestrian enhancements appropriate for the
roadway conditions. Sidewalks at intersections provide pedestrians with a refuge area while waiting
to cross the street. Additionally, enhancements such as adding marked crosswalks, installing
enhanced crossing treatments, or signalizing an intersection help to create more pedestrian-friendly
intersections. The specific type of treatment at a crossing may range from simple, marked
crosswalks to full traffic signals or grade-separated crossings. Appendix B provides examples and
design guidance of different crossing treatments Glendale should consider.

Figure 5.3 illustrates the locations in need of pedestrian intersection improvements and Table 5.3
provides examples of these intersection needs. Appendix A provides a full listing of improvement
recommendations, their priority scoring, and a planning level cost estimate.

A pedestrian network is only as strong as its weakest link. Sporadic corridor and business
development have caused small gaps in the existing pedestrian network, which creates barriers to
pedestrian travel. Strategically filling these gaps can link people to jobs, shopping, recreational
opportunities, and provide choices for convenient travel by foot. Figure 5.4 illustrates the locations
sidewalk infill improvements by their priority scoring and Table 5.4 provides examples of these infill
needs. Appendix A provides a full listing of improvement recommendations, their priority scoring,
and a planning level cost estimate.

108 | Final Report
Railroad Crossings
Railroads often act as barriers in the active transportation network by limiting the pedestrian and
cyclist’s ability to access a location. Providing sidewalk, bicycle, and ADA facilities at railroad
crossings aids in connecting pedestrians and cyclists to destinations. Figure 5.5 presents the priority
scoring for locations that may benefit from pedestrian crossing enhancements. It’s important to note
that any improvements to railroad crossings requires close coordination, review, assessment, and
buy-in from the railroad operator. Warrants for at-grade crossing infrastructure also requires an
assessment of train frequency, pedestrian and bicycle level, and on-the-ground conditions to
determine if additional active warning devices or grade separation is required. The MAG Bicycle
and Pedestrian Pathway/Railroad Crossing Recommendations report provides additional
information in regard to potential treatments and design guidelines for at-grade railroad crossings.

Transit Connections
Connecting bus stops to nearby active transportation facilities addresses first/last mile issues by
facilitating walking and bicycling to and from the public transportation network. Figure 5.5 present
the priority scoring for transit stops that require pedestrian facility connections. Partnerships can be
made with Valley Metro to enhance and expand accessibility to bus stops located along Glendale-
maintained roadways. Additionally, evaluation and need for mid-block crossing and transit stops,
particularly high ridership stops, should be considered.

Accessibility for People with Disabilities
A facility's compliance to the ADA not only affects individuals with disabilities, but all pedestrians.
Maintaining a smooth sidewalk surface free of obstructions helps to eliminate tripping hazards,
especially for visually impaired pedestrians, and makes the path easier to traverse, particularly for
persons using a wheelchair, cane, or other mobility assistance device. Furthermore, curb ramps,
and particularly detectable warning surfaces, create a safe transition for blind and low-vision
persons and provide a vital safety cue that you are entering a roadway. Currently Glendale adheres
to ADA standards for all new construction and alterations to existing public rights-of-way, as
outlined in Glendale Engineering Standard Details. ADA standards include, but are not limited to:
    • A 5 FT x 5 FT passing zone is required every 200 FT on sidewalks with widths less than 5 FT;
         and
    • Curb ramps must be 36 inches in width or greater, meet cross and running slope standards,
         and have a high-visibility truncated dome present.
As part of the ATP process, an aerial assessment of sidewalk conditions, as well as the location and
conditions of curb ramps, was conducted to assist Glendale in identifying locations where sidewalk
and curb ramp upgrades may be needed for ADA compliance. The results of the inventory found:
   •   Sidewalks were primarily less than 5 FT in width;
   •   9,176 curb ramps do not have a detectable warning or are in poor condition; and
   •   10,077 driveways had potential ADA compliance issues.
The findings from the ATP should be utilized as a base for implementing citywide ADA
improvements. Detailed, on the ground assessments are needed to confirm ADA compliance as
additional factors impact a facility’s compliance. Where possible, upgrades to facilities to conform
with ADA standards should be incorporated into maintenance programs and programmed
improvements. For all future improvements, facilities should be constructed in compliance with ADA
standards.

Bicycle Network Improvement Recommendations
A comprehensive bikeway network improves bicyclists’ level of safety, convenience, and access to
key destinations. Planning a bikeway network enables Glendale to prioritize and seek funding to
construct bicycle facilities where they will provide the greatest benefit to bicyclists and the
community-at-large. The recommended bicycle network presented in this section was developed
with five main objectives:
    •    Define bicycle facilities and create consistent use of treatments and application so users can
         plan trips based on their comfort level;
    •    Logically connect existing facilities to improve local and regional nonmotorized mobility;
    •    Identify low-cost solutions that fit within the existing roadway footprint;
    •    Determine upgrades to accommodate all ages and abilities; and
    •    Identify long-term, regional needs.
Addressing all identified recommendations creates a robust regional bicycle network that provides a
more comfortable riding experience for experienced bicyclists, and low-stress options for children
and those not as confident riding a bike.

Table 5.5 provides examples of recommended upgrades to current on-street bicycle facilities to
further improve a bicyclists level of comfort and improve messaging. Figure 5.6 illustrates the
locations of upgrade recommendations. Appendix A provides a full listing of improvement
recommendations, their priority scoring, and a planning level cost estimate, while Appendix B
provides examples and design guidance of different bicycle facilities Glendale should consider.

New Bicycle Facilities
People who ride bicycles vary in their physical abilities, experience levels, and comfort level riding
adjacent to motor vehicles. The identified new bicycle facility recommendations include gaps in the
current network that limit a person’s ability to bike to their destination. In addition, opportunities to
expand Glendale’s bicycle network and create regional recreation routes were also identified.
Filling in these network gaps links hundreds of thousands of residents to jobs and provides choices
for convenient travel by bicycle.

Bicycle Facilities on Arterials
Arterials are the backbone of the Phoenix metropolitan’s regional transportation network. In most of
the region, arterials provide the only through-route between cities and across rivers, canals,
mountains, and freeways. While the Glendale ATP focuses on creating a network for persons of all
ages and abilities, accommodating bicycle travel along arterials not only provides regional access,
but also provides direct connections to the places people want to travel – shopping centers,
businesses, jobs, grocery stores, medical facilities, and transit stops. Specific bike treatments and
the feasibility of installing bicycle facilities should be considered to accommodate both the
experienced, commuter bicyclist and the less experienced rider. Key arterials that should be
evaluated for potential bicycle accommodation include:
    •   Olive Ave: 67th Ave to Glendale Community College. Key transit corridor that connects the
        popular 67th Avenue transit route to Glendale Community College.
    •   67th Ave, 59th Ave, 43rd Ave (Phoenix maintained), Bethany Home Rd, Glendale Ave,
        Northern Ave, Peoria Ave, Thunderbird Rd, and Bell Rd. High bicycle crash corridors and
        the highest ridership transit routes in the City. Arterials, such as Bell Road, pose numerous
        design constraints to incorporate bike facilities.
Arterials have higher traffic volumes than local streets, and typically have traffic signals at major
intersections. Preferred treatments on arterials include barrier-protected bike lanes, cycle tracks,
and buffered bike lanes. On many arterial streets, accommodating bicycle facilities requires major
investment and reallocating space within the existing right-of-way.

Final Report | 119

Accommodating Bicyclists Within Current Cross Sections
Glendale’s existing roadway cross section options includes limited bicycle facilities. Low-cost
upgrades to accommodate bicyclists within current cross-sections can be accommodated in some
locations by:
    •    Restriping and lane narrowing within the existing pavement width. Implementation of these
         improvements are best achieved during roadway resurfacing or restriping projects.
    •    Reallocating space for cross sections’ elements within the existing right-of-way to realize
         higher quality active transportation facilities while considering the needs of motorized users.

Local Cross Section
This represents a typical residential street in Glendale. Lanes and parking areas are usually not
delineated. Per the City’s 2015 Standard Details, reduced pavement widths may be allowed. To
accommodate the needs of bicyclists, the modified cross sections below provide a range of
potential configurations.

Collector Cross Section
Collectors are designed to accommodate motor vehicle traffic between local, residential roads and
busier, wider roads. Collectors typically create low-stress connections for bicyclists to travel. Per the
City’s 2015 Standard Details, reduced pavement widths may be allowed. To accommodate the
needs of bicyclists, the modified cross sections below provide a range of potential configurations.




                                                                                          Final Report | 123
On-Street Parking Cross Section
Currently, Glendale has over 90 centerline miles of roadways classified as “Shoulder Stripe with
Parking”. These routes are classified as and signed as “Bike Route Parked Vehicles”, which creates
a shared shoulder for bicyclists and parked vehicles. Sharing bicycle facilities with parked vehicles
can create an unsafe and uncomfortable riding experience for most cyclists, as they may need to
weave in and out of traffic to avoid parked vehicles. To accommodate bicycle facilities on streets
with parked vehicles, the following cross sections can be applied. For streets with on-street parking
that do not have businesses, homes, or parks located along the route, current shoulder striping
could be easily signed as a bike lane and on-street parking removed.




124 | Final Report
Arterial Cross Section
Arterials are vital to Glendale’s transportation network, as they provide direct access to jobs,
shopping centers, major destinations, and regional facilities and also provide the only access
across canals and rivers. Retrofitting Glendale’s arterial network with bicycle facilities can help
expand the regional bicycle network and provide necessary commuting routes to jobs and transit
stops. The cross sections presented below provide several options to fit within Glendale’s many
arterial cross sections.

Bicycle Treatments at Intersections
Bike lanes define a portion of the roadway that is preferential or exclusive for use by bicyclists;
allowing bicyclists to travel alongside traffic. In addition, bike lanes remind motorists to look for
bicyclists when turning and that bicyclists have the right to the road. A bike lane along a corridor
may provide a comfortable riding experience; however, if that designated space disappears at an
intersection, the entire corridor may no longer be attractive to bicyclists. As noted in Chapter 3,
intersections are common locations of conflict between drivers and bicyclists. Therefore, particular
attention to intersections is necessary in order to increase motorist awareness, provide bicyclists with
a sense of comfort, and improve the riding experience along a corridor. Figure 5.8 illustrates
locations where intersection improvements are needed to extend bicycle facilities through
intersections to alert motorists of bicycle traffic. Appendix A provides a full listing of improvement
recommendations, their priority scoring, and a planning level cost estimate.

River, Canal, Freeway Crossings
Rivers, canals, and freeways create barriers in the active transportation network. Crossings at these
facilities are needed to provide a continuous pedestrian and bicycle network. Study roadways were
evaluated to identify locations that currently lack bicycle connectivity at river, canal, and freeway
crossings. In addition, locations that may warrant crossings if development occurs were identified.
Figure 5.8 and Table 5.7 presents example of locations that may benefit from crossing facilities.

Near-Term and Quick Win Projects

years to complete. To build momentum behind the ATP, short-term actions that the City should focus
on to begin implementing. These near-term/quick win projects were selected based on a variety of
factors, such as priority scoring, ease of implementation, constructability, potential benefit to
socioeconomically disadvantaged population groups, and strong public or stakeholder interest.

students and employees traveling via walking, biking, transit, and vehicle to the college daily. This
high activity zone has historically experienced significant pedestrian and bicycle safety issues. Active
transportation improvements such as improving safety conditions at entrances and creating new
bicycle facility connections can help improve safety and accessibility for students and employees.

Safety Improvements
Safety concerns often deter people from choosing to walk or bike. With increased emphasis on the
benefits of active transportation, proving safe environments is increasingly important. Overcoming
these barriers is possible – with education and strategic improvements to the built environment,
more people will view walking and biking as safe and convenient options. The following presents
recommended targeted safety infrastructure improvements to address corridors and intersections
that have historically had the highest rates of pedestrian- and bicycle-related crashes in the City.

Canal and Trail Improvements and Maintenance
In addition to providing new and enhanced facilities, it is imperative that the City maintain its
existing canal and trail facilities that provide vital recreational and regional active transportation
links. Through numerous stakeholder and community outreach events, a variety of safety and
maintenance concerns were identified. Recommended canal and trail maintenance and safety
improvements include:
   •   Ongoing replacement of broken lights, trimming of overgrown shrubs and vegetation, and
       regular pavement maintenance to keep the trails in good repair for biking, walking and
       other trail activities. Specific locations in need of maintenance/repair are:
           o Skunk Creek Trail/59th Ave – replace broken lights
           o Sunset Vita Park – overgrown trees limit sight distance
           o Arizona Canal Trail/75th Ave – overgrown shrubs limit sight distance
           o Skunk Creek Trail/Union Hills Dr – overgrown shrubs limit sight distance
   •   Address safety concerns that may create a barrier for persons to use a trail, including:
           o Skunk Creek Trail near Foothills Branch Library – steep curve with no lighting near
             library causes safety concerns
           o Skunk Creek Trail/69th Ave – sharp, steep curve causes safety concerns
           o Arizona Canal Trail/59th Ave – no pedestrian lighting
           o Arizona Canal Trail/51st Ave – underpass perceived as unsafe



Equestrian Accommodations
Glendale has had a long history and culture as an equestrian-friendly community with numerous
horse properties and equestrian trails. Keeping Glendale horse friendly is a priority of many
residents. To maintain and improve safety and connectivity for horseback riders in Glendale, the
City should continue to invest in providing equine friendly roads, trails, and signals. Recommended
equestrian accommodations include:
   •   At equestrian trail crossings, standard equestrian crossing signs at all at-grade road
       crossings should be installed to alert motorists.
   •   At arterial crossings along the Bridle Path and at key canal crossings, equestrian waiting
       areas and two push-button signal actuators that allow both pedestrians and equestrian
       riders to be detected should be considered. Most seated riders can operate a push button
       that is between 5 FT and 6 FT. Equestrian waiting areas allow horses to stand back from
       traffic until it is safe to cross a road. Consider expanding the width of the trail surface before
       it meets the road, forming a rectangular or fan-shaped waiting area.
Provide equestrian staging areas at key trailheads to allow equestrians a place to park and access
regional routes. Potential new equestrian staging areas to evaluate include: 67th Ave at the Arizona
Canal Trail; Greenway Rd and Acoma Dr; 51st Ave south of the Arizona Canal; and off Skunk
Creek Trail.
