                     6. NEXT STEPS




146 | Final Report
Active Transportation Recommendations
Improving conditions for walking and biking is an important priority for Glendale. Implementation
of the ATP will require leadership and dedication for the investment and development of active
transportation facilities. To make the ATP’s vision a reality, Glendale should focus on the following
overarching active transportation recommendations.




               The transportation network should provide safe, connected infrastructure
               including roads, sidewalks, bicycle facilities, parking, trails and paths, and bus
               stops throughout the City. Barriers that inhibit pedestrian and bicycle travel
               should be addressed to provide more options to reach a destination.



               Glendale should dedicate a full-time staff member to champion the goals of
               the ATP and lead active transportation planning efforts for the City. The staff
               member would be reasonable for implementing improvements identified in the
               ATP, seeking funding sources, attending committee meetings, and
               coordinating with City department to incorporate ATP recommendations.




               Educate all road users about their rights and responsibilities regarding traffic
               laws. Ensure that all road users understand the proper uses for bicycle and
               pedestrian infrastructure. Work with the police, schools, and community
               partners to increase awareness of traffic safety through educational outreach
               and media campaigns.



               Glendale should seek opportunities to work with other jurisdictions and public,
               private, and non-profit sectors to fund, plan, build, and implement this plan.
               Glendale should also support regional active transportation efforts through
               data sharing, attending committee meetings, and providing technical
               guidance as needed.



               Make active transportation more attractive and accessible to a variety of users
               - commuters, residents, recreationalists, equestrians, visitors, and people of all
               ages and abilities. Marketing bicycle and pedestrian attractions, infrastructure,
               and resources generates awareness and interest among potential users.
               Increased usage of active transportation modes supports economic
               development, healthy lifestyle choices, and community-building.




                                                                                        Final Report | 147
How to Implement This Plan
The completion of the ATP is the first of many necessary steps for Glendale to achieve its active
transportation goals. Implementation of this plan is a long-term effort that requires dedication and
commitment by Glendale staff. As shown in Figure 6.1, to implement the ATP, Glendale should
follow a five-step process that includes:
    •    Incorporate the ATP: The ATP represents the ideas, issues, and needs of hundreds of public
         participants, as well as a shared vision for active transportation in Glendale.
    •    Determine Feasibility: Priority needs should go through a comprehensive assessment to
         determine what type of facility is best suited for the area, if the project is economically
         viable, and to conduct a fatal flaw review of environmental, right-of-way, utility, and other
         issues that may delay implementation.
    •    Recommend Programming: High priority, feasible improvements should be programmed, and
         Glendale staff should begin seeking partnerships and funding opportunities for long-term
         improvements. When applicable, pedestrian and bicycle improvements should be
         incorporated into regularly scheduled maintenance activities. Project bundling allows
         Glendale to address multiple gaps/issues at the same time by efficiently leveraging public
         funds, as well as improving funding chances.
    •    Develop Annual Project List: Projects should be reassessed and evaluated every year to create
         an annual project list that identifies priority active transportation investments.
    •    Track and Evaluate: Annually evaluate and report on the progress of the ATP. As the region
         grows and technologies and designs change, recommendations in the ATP should be
         reevaluated at least every five years.

Figure 6.1. Next Steps




148 | Final Report
How to Determine Feasibility
A range of factors can influence a pedestrian and bicyclists’ comfort and safety. Selecting the
appropriate pedestrian and bicycle facility for a roadway must balance traffic conditions, land use
context, maintenance cost, and implementation cost.

Design Resources
A number of federal and state resources are available for planning and design. In addition to
Glendale’s roadway design standards, design resources include:
    • AASHTO Guide for the Development of Bicycle Facilities
    • AASHTO Guide for the Planning, Design, and Operation of Pedestrian Facilities
    • FHWA Achieving Multimodal Networks
    • FHWA Separated Bike Lane Planning and Design Guide
    • FHWA Incorporating On-Road Bicycle Networks into Resurfacing Projects
    • NACTO Designing for All Ages and Abilities
    • NACTO Urban Bikeway Design Guide
    • NACTO Urban Street Design Guide
    • MAG Active Transportation Plan
    • MAG Bicycle and Pedestrian Pathway/Railroad Crossing Recommendations
    • MAG Complete Streets Guide

Roadway Modification Strategies
Many of Glendale’s roadways provide adequate right-of-way to accommodate sidewalks, bike
lanes or paved shoulders; however, additional right-of-way or modifications may be needed to
accommodate wider or more protected facilities. As mentioned in Chapter 3, examples of roadway
modification strategies to incorporate active transportation facilities include:
• Restriping and lane narrowing within the existing pavement width. Implementation of these
  improvements are best achieved during roadway resurfacing or restriping projects.
• Reallocating space for cross sections’ elements within the existing right-of-way to realize higher
  quality active transportation facilities while considering the needs of motorized users.
• Locating sidewalks behind a drainage swale to avoid installing curb and gutter when there is
  insufficient room between the roadway and the swale.
• Widening roadways in areas where other modification strategies are not feasible. Additional
  right-of-way may be needed to accommodate pedestrian and bicycle facilities. Pavement widths
  at intersections and midblock pedestrian crossings, however, should be minimized to reduce
  pedestrian crossing distances.




                                                                                       Final Report | 149
Maintenance Considerations
Maintaining the active transportation network once it has
been implemented preserves Glendale’s investment and
helps support the transportation needs of residents.
Bicyclists and pedestrians are vulnerable to
pavement/sidewalk irregularities such as cracks,
potholes, broken glass, sand, etc. Unmaintained
landscaping causes safety issues by obstructing bicycle
lanes and sidewalks and blocking visibility. Major storms
and motor vehicle crashes can leave debris, presenting
hazards to pedestrians and bicyclists, which must be
picked up as soon as possible.

Maintenance needs are typically identified through one
of three sources: the public reporting a problem, routine
inspections, or special inspections after a storm, crash,
or construction project. Maintenance activities can
generally be categorized into one of two types:
    •    Routine maintenance: performed annually or
         more frequently
    •    Major or capital maintenance: involves more
         intensive activities such as pavement seal coating,
         pavement overlays, or pavement reconstruction,
         or structural rehabilitation or replacement
Glendale should monitor scheduled maintenance programs to ensure bicycle and pedestrian
facility maintenance. Buffered sidewalks and shared use paths often require more frequent and
different maintenance practices (depending on the degree and type of physical separation). During
the facility design selection phase of project development, maintenance needs and costs should be
considered. Routine maintenance best practices include:
    •    Maintaining structure quality through spot repairs, regular overlays, and long-term
         rehabilitation
    •    Sweeping and removal of garbage and debris
    •    Trimming overgrown vegetation
    •    Restriping pavement markings as needed
    •    Repairing damage caused by monsoons, crashes, and other unforeseen events
    •    Repairing and replacing signage




150 | Final Report
Living Laboratory
In order to achieve the goals and vision
established in the ATP, Glendale must be willing
to be creative and innovative in the design,
funding, and implementation of sidewalks and
bikeways. Tactical urbanism, also known as
living laboratory, has been embraced as a low-
cost alternative to implementing both temporary
and permanent pedestrian and bicycle projects.
This method of testing out longer-term
infrastructure improvements sprung out of a
series of citizen led efforts to “take action when Morgan Hill, CA conducted a two-day demonstration project to
confronted with the slow pace of change.” It can test the implementation of a buffered bike lane on Monterey
                                                   Road through their downtown core.
take shape in many forms, ranging from smaller
“guerilla interventions” to demonstration projects
led by both community groups and cities.
Examples of methods include pavement
markings, pop-up bikeways, adding furniture
and/or seating to create public space, and using
planters as barriers for a protected bike lane.

Glendale should embrace tactical urbanism as a
strategy of implementing pedestrian and bicycle
improvements. Tactical urbanism projects have
low costs and have the potential to garner
excitement around active transportation. Tactical
urbanism projects also allow residents to                  Bentonville, AR tested three projects throughout the city to
envision an environment with improved active               determine the feasibility and evaluate the usage of two-way
transportation facilities and to test out                  protected bike lanes at key locations. Cyclists and motorists were
improvements. Several grant programs exist to              surveyed to understand issues, concerns, and to evaluate support
fund living laboratories, including:                       for the project.

    • Walton Family Foundation
    • John S. and James L. Knight Foundation
    • National Association of Realtors

Develop Project Bundles
Project “bundling” is a concept that
refers to grouping adjacent
improvements into a single active
transportation improvement project
– for instance, combining
intersection, sidewalk gap, and
bicycle gap improvements along a
section of roadway to provide a complete active transportation network along the corridor. Project
bundling allows Glendale to address multiple gaps/issues at the same time by efficiently leveraging
public funds, as well as improving funding chances. Appendix A provides detailed information on
potential project bundles.


                                                                                                            Final Report | 151
Developing the Annual Project List
Ultimately, the ATP presents a high-level, planning-scale evaluation of improvement needs in
Glendale. All identified improvement needs presented in the ATP requires a comprehensive
assessment process that includes:
    •    Constructability Audit – potential projects will need to undergo a thorough feasibility review
         of right-of-way, environmental, design, and cost constraints

    •    Coordination Review – identify potential stakeholder and/or private partnership
         opportunities

Once prioritized and evaluated, the list of projects will need to go through an annual review
process to create an annual active transportation project list based on available funding. To
communicate with the public and maintain a transparent project selection process, a status tracker
to clearly convey the status of the projects is recommended. The flowchart below provides guidance
on the annual project list development process.




152 | Final Report
How to Track and Evaluate Progress
Performance measures help to track the ATP’s progress and effectiveness over time. Table 6.1
outlines a wide range of suggested performance measures to assess the success of the ATP, track
changes in the built environment, identify trends in travel behaviors, and provide performance
information to decision-makers. Tracking and reporting the progress of performance measures
provides more transparency while building momentum and public support.

                                                                                                       Final Report | 153
Bicycle and Pedestrian Counts
Currently, Glendale does not collect data to track how often pedestrian and bike facilities are used.
Collecting data will enable the City to evaluate trends, such as increase/decrease in usage, peak
travel periods, and high activity locations. Better data on pedestrian and cyclist travel can help to
determine where investments are most needed. It also helps to quantify the benefits of walking and
biking. Ultimately, better data will make active transportation projects more competitive for funding
opportunities. Glendale should coordinate with MAG to include priority locations within Glendale
to be included in future regional pedestrian and bicycle counts program. The City should establish
a bike and pedestrian counts program, in combination with existing counts collection, to track and
monitor the number of pedestrian and bicyclists that use facilities in Glendale. A number of
counters are available that track bicyclist and pedestrian activity. Types of technology available to
conduct counts are: wireless sensors, thermal imaging, app based counting software, etc.

League of American Bicyclists
Since 1995, the League of American Bicyclists have awarded “Bicycle Friendly Community” (BFC)
and “Walk Friendly Community” designations to communities across the Nation. As of 2018, more
than 850 communities have applied, and 431 have been awarded a Bronze, Silver, Gold or
Platinum designation. Glendale submitted an application for the BFC program in 2012 and
received an Honorable Mention for having some of the key building blocks of creating a BFC in
place. The completion of the ATP moves the City one step closer to being awarded a BFC. To be
awarded with a Bronze-level BFC award, Glendale should take the following steps and measures:
    •    Adopt a Complete Streets policy and guidance to ensure that roadways accommodate all
         users, regardless of age, ability, or mode of transportation.
    •    Continue to expand the bicycle network to achieve 25 percent of all total roadway mileage
         having a bicycle facility, 20 percent of high-speed roadways with a bicycle facility, and
         increased access to public transportation.
    •    Address major pedestrian and bicycle safety concerns and work with law enforcement to
         promote safety and bicycling skills
    •    Hire a full-time Active Transportation Program Manager dedicate to work on active
         transportation issues and concerns.
    •    Incorporate bicycle facility design guidance and standards into the City’s roadway design
         standards.
    •    Develop local ordinance to ensure that bicyclists are protected (i.e., parking in bike lanes,
         helmet usage, etc.).
    •    Continue to participate in Bike Month and Bike to Work events.
    •    Provide pedestrian and bicycle education opportunities to schools, children, adults, and the
         general public.
    •    The City has participated in Safe Routes to School programs in the past and should continue
         and enhance a consistent bicycle education program at more than 50% of local elementary,
         middle, and high schools.




154 | Final Report
Funding Ideas
Various funding sources are available to fund maintenance of existing sidewalks and bicycle
facilities, construct new sidewalks and shared use paths, and to develop new on-street bicycle
facilities. Most funding sources are competitive and require the preparation of applications. Some
applications may be more competitive if Glendale collaborates with local and regional agencies to
jointly prepare and team on the construction of improvements. Table 6.2 provides a list of funding
sources that may be applicable for needs identified in the ATP.

Federal, State, and Local Funding
Federal funds from the Surface Transportation Block Grant Program (STBGP) are allocated to the
state and distributed proportional to population, allowing funding to get to as many different types
of communities as possible. Program funding for transportation alternatives (TA) is included within
the STBGP. Federal transportation spending can vary and tends be dependent on economic
factors. Glendale should be creative in obtaining different sources of funding in order to implement
projects. Municipal funds may also be used to construct bicycle and pedestrian facilities.

Roadway Safety Assessments (RSAs)
Road Safety Assessments (RSAs) are an active approach to improving road safety. Annually, MAG
sets aside $300,000 to complete RSAs for member agencies. Glendale should pursue obtaining
MAG RSA funds to conducted studies at key corridors and intersections identified in Chapter 5. The
RSAs can help produce designs that reduce the number and severity of crashes, identify key human
factor considerations, and integrate multimodal roadway safety concerns.

Public Private Partnership (P3)
Public Private Partnerships (P3s) are contractual agreements that can leverage funds from both
sectors for infrastructure projects and facilities. Where municipal budgets fall short, private revenue
can fill the gaps. Agreements may include funding, design, construction, operation, and/or
management with terms agreed upon by the two entities.

Developers
It is anticipated that as new residential and commercial developments arise, developers will build
additional facilities that will expand and enhance the active transportation network including:
   •   Constructing pedestrian and bicycle facilities to their property and, as applicable, provide
       connections to nearest facilities.
   •   Ensuring pedestrian connectivity through the end of cul-de-sacs to shorten trip distances for
       walking and bicycling.
   •   Installing shaded and secure bicycle parking facilities at residential, office, school,
       commercial, and recreational developments.

156 | Final Report
Policy and Program Considerations
In addition to improving Glendale’s active transportation network through engineering solutions, a
comprehensive approach that integrates policy, programmatic, education, enforcement, and
encouragement elements is the most effective approach to creating and sustaining a pedestrian-
and bicycle-friendly network. The following are policy and program concepts to consider.




              • Update the City’s Capital Improvement Programs to incorporate the projects
                and programs recommended in the ATP.
              • Upgrade roadway design standards to include bicycle facilities.
              • Include paint striping maintenance for bicycle facilities as an annual, on-going
                line items in the City’s transportation budget.
              • Review and coordinated re-paving schedule to identify opportunities to include
                active transportation improvements.
              • Program and seek funding to implement high priority improvement projects,
                particularly those that provide direct connections to critical facilities such as
                schools, employment centers, and public transportation. As funding is secured,
                engineering assessments will be needed to identify what type of facility is most
                suitable.
              • During the design process, evaluate flexible street designs that allow Glendale
                to install a range of pedestrian and bicycle facility types to complement the
                corridor and surrounding land uses.
              • Partner with Valley Metro to provide amenities at bus stops and to improve
                pedestrian and bike connections to transit stops, regional transit centers, and
                park-and-ride lots
              • Retrofit existing facilities to incorporate ADA compliant facilities, as needed.
              • Explore options and policies to include electric bikes and scooters within the
                active transportation system.
              • In high pedestrian activity areas, evaluate pedestrian crossing times to ensure
                that pedestrians have ample time to cross.
              • Recommend a minimum green time at intersections that do not possess
                pedestrian or bicycle detection and have a high ped/bike usage. Shorter green
                times should be utilized only when ped/bike detection is available but not
                activated by a pedestrian/bicyclist.
              • Implement a bike rack program which allows for bike parking to be provided
                within the street right-of-way.
              • Work with the Parks and Recreation Department to install signage on routes that
                connect to parks and trailheads.
              • Collaborate with MAG and local jurisdictions to incorporate wayfinding for
                regional bicycle routes.


                                                                                        Final Report | 157
                     • Develop maps and guides that show existing networks and highlight walking and
                       bicycling routes for accessing key destinations throughout the city.
                     • Consider implementing a Glendale Active Transportation Program with a focus
                       on education, training, and promotion of active transportation. Educational
                       outreach might focus:
                          • Schools, perhaps as part of Safe Routes to Schools program
                          • Bicycle skills training and safety awareness training for all roadway users
                          • Bicycle repair workshops
                     • Create an Active Transportation webpage to host maps, education efforts, and
                       information on planned and completed active transportation improvements.
                     • Partner with other jurisdiction and community groups to develop regional
                       campaigns that address unsafe and illegal behaviors of motorists, pedestrians,
                       and bicyclists while encouraging mutual respect among all road users and
                       encouraging active transportation.
                     • Feature rotating traffic safety and educational messages on the City’s website
                       and social media channels.
                     • Champion and support regional initiatives aimed at driver awareness of
                       pedestrians and bicycles, particularly at intersections with high pedestrian and
                       bicycle related crashes.
                     • Support or partner with advocacy groups such as Coalition of Arizona Bicyclists
                       in offering bicycle-skills trainings for children and adults.




                     • Support Bicycle Safety Month in May and Back to School Safety Month in August
                       by organizing local events throughout Glendale.
                     • Sponsor “Open Street” events once a year. Open Street events involve the
                       closure of section of a road for a half or full day and allows people to enjoy
                       walking and biking in a car-free environment.
                     • Support the “Bicycle Friendly Community” and “Bicycle Friendly Business”
                       designation program through the League of American Bicyclists.
                     • Host launch parties when new bicycle facilities have been constructed.
                     • Provide bikes and gear such as helmets, bike locks, or bike lights to encourage
                       safe riding. Giveaways are most commonly used to incentivize or reward
                       participation in another program activity.




158 | Final Report
• Partner with Police Department to strategize and implement targeted
  enforcement, possibly in areas with high number of crashes or near schools.
• During future planning projects, encourage project participation of local law
  enforcement in stakeholder meetings, where they can provide insight into trends
  and issues. During the meetings, active transportation professionals and
  advisory groups can also provide insight on unsafe behaviors to help police
  offers evaluate the best methods of enforcement.
• Implement a comprehensive safety campaign that includes education,
  encouragement, and enforcement components.
• Support MAG’s inter-agency working group that brings together staff from MAG
  member agencies and other partners to strategize about where targeted
  enforcement efforts are most needed.
• Publicize any enforcement actions through partners and media in order to
  achieve maximum driver behavior change.
• Continue to train officers in bicycle safety enforcement issues and principles.




• Establish an annual bicycle and pedestrian count review program to determine
  baseline mode share conditions and subsequent changes.
• Develop a set of performance measures to annually evaluate and report on the
  progress of the ATP. Performance metrics can be compiled into a Bicycle and
  Pedestrian Report Card to easily illustrate accomplishments and general trends.

Planning cost estimates include:
    • Signed Bike Route - costs for sign placement every ½ mile only
    • Shared Lanes - costs for pavement markings placement every 250 FT only
    • Bike Lane-
             o Bike lanes fit within current configuration – includes cost to add bike lanes in both directions
             o Corridor less than 0.50 miles – includes obliterating existing striping and restriping corridor
             o Corridor greater than 0.50 miles – includes slurry seal or micro seal and restriping corridor
             o Modifications to raised medias included in planning cost estimates
             o Costs do not include right-of-way acquisition, utility relocation, additional pavement, etc.

Planning cost estimates include:
    • Signed Bike Route - costs for sign placement every ½ mile only
    • Shared Lanes - costs for pavement markings placement every 250 FT only
    • Bike Lane-
             o Bike lanes fit within current configuration – includes cost to add bike lanes in both directions
             o Corridor less than 0.50 miles – includes obliterating existing striping and restriping corridor
             o Corridor greater than 0.50 miles – includes slurry seal or micro seal and restriping corridor
             o Modifications to raised medias included in planning cost estimates
             o Costs do not include right-of-way acquisition, utility relocation, additional pavement, etc.

Typical cost estimates for striping thermoplastic pavement markings range from $0.50 to $2.00 per
linear foot. At signalized intersections with high volumes of bicycles and/or motor vehicles,
especially those with frequent bicyclist left-turns and/or motorist right-turns, bike boxes should be
considered for installation. Bike boxes typically cost $5,000 per box with on-going pavement
maintenance requirements. Bike boxes should be painted green to help bicyclists and motorists
visually identify potential conflict areas and encourage safe driving and bicycling behavior.

                                                                                                             Final Report | A-36
Project Bundles
Project “bundling” is a concept
that refers to grouping adjacent
improvements into a single active
transportation improvement
project – for instance, combining
intersection, sidewalk gap, and
bicycle gap improvements along a
section of roadway to provide a
complete active transportation network along the corridor. Project bundling allows Glendale to
address multiple gaps/issues at the same time by efficiently leveraging public funds, as well as
improving funding chances.

It is important to remember that all projects within the proposed network have merit and should be
implemented as timely and cost-effective opportunities arise. The results of the prioritization process
are not intended to preclude projects from receiving funding or from being incorporated into a new
development or other roadway project.

                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     Final Report |A- 46
       Appendix B.
Facility Design Guidance
The Design Guidelines of the Glendale Active Transportation Plan are intended to assist in
designing transportation systems that serve all users. The guidelines will help to implement the
Glendale Active Transportation Plan and create a community with accessible and comfortable
active transportation options for people of all ages and abilities. The design recommendations are
not intended to impose standards, but to offer guidance. The guidelines listed here are a collection
of national and regional design resources, best practices, methods, and guidelines which help the
design of facilities and amenities. This list is not exhaustive. Glendale should consider consulting
publications directly to ensure that the most current version is used. Arizona Department of
Transportation and Maricopa Association of Governments should also be consulted, especially
when improvements are proposed on a state-owned roadway.

Pedestrian Facilities and Treatments
To create a pedestrian network that works for all users, a series of facilities and treatments are
needed to create a connected, complete, and accessible network. The following section outlines
potential pedestrian facilities and treatment types to assist Glendale in the selection and design of
pedestrian facilities.

 Sidewalk
                                    Sidewalks are the most fundamental element of the pedestrian
                                    network. Providing adequate and accessible facilities can lead
                                    to increased numbers of people walking, improved safety, and
                                    the creation of social space. When retrofitting gaps in the
                                    sidewalk network, locations near transit stops, schools, parks,
                                    public buildings, and other areas with high concentrations of
                                    pedestrians should be the highest priority.
 Design Guidance and Considerations
      •   6 FT sidewalks on collector or arterial roadways. A pedestrian through zone width of 6 FT
          enables two pedestrians (including wheelchair users) to travel side-by-side, or to pass
          each other comfortably.
      •   In areas of high demand, sidewalks should contain adequate width to accommodate high
          volumes and different walking speeds of pedestrians. For example, a minimum 8 FT wide
          sidewalk (10 FT preferred) is recommended adjacent to schools.
      •   5 FT minimum in areas with low pedestrian volume and constrained right-of-way.
      •   Should be built to Americans with Disabilities Act (ADA) standards.
      •   High quality transitions/crossings are advised such as refuge islands, rectangular rapid
          flashing beacons, pedestrian hybrid beacons, high visibility crosswalks, lighting, etc.
      •   Minimum sidewalk clear widths should be free of sign posts, utility and signal cabinets,
          pull boxes and poles, mailboxes, fire hydrants and other obstructions.
 Planning Cost Estimates
    • $30 – $40 per Linear Foot (LF)
           o Price varies by the size of the project
           o Does not include cost for right-of-way acquisition, utility relocation, and other
               ancillary costs
           o Does not include 10% contingency and 20% design


B-1 | Final Report
 Shared Use Path
                                     Shared use paths are paved facilities physically separated from
                                     motorized vehicular traffic by an open space or barrier. Shared use
                                     paths are used by bicyclists, pedestrians, skaters, runners and
                                     others. Since shared use paths serve as pedestrian facilities, they
                                     must comply with ADA standards.
 Design Guidance and Considerations
    • 10 FT minimum width is recommended in most situations and will be adequate for moderate
        to heavy use. 12 FT width is recommended for heavy use situations with high concentrations
        of multiple users such as joggers, bicyclists, rollerbladers and pedestrians.
    • Shared use paths along roadways are best in areas with few access points or roadways
        crossing the path. Where a shared use path terminates, special consideration should be
        given to transitions so as not to encourage unsafe wrong way riding by bicyclists.
 Planning Cost Estimates
    • $60 – $80 per LF
            o Price varies by the size of the project
            o Does not include 10% contingency, 20% design, and costs for right-of-way
               acquisition, utility relocation, and other ancillary costs

Crossings and Intersections
Crossings are potential conflict points between pedestrians and vehicles and thus require design
treatments that set clear expectations for all roadway users. Every intersection in Glendale should
be designed for pedestrian safety and comfort, with pedestrian enhancements appropriate for the
roadway conditions. The specific type of treatment at a crossing may range from simple, marked
crosswalks to full traffic signals or grade-separated crossings. Crosswalk lines should not be used
indiscriminately, and appropriate selection and implementation of crossing treatments should be
evaluated by an engineer or an engineering study before installation.

 Marked Crosswalk
                              A marked crosswalk signals to motorists that they must stop for
                              pedestrians and encourages pedestrians to cross at designated
                              locations. Installing crosswalks alone, however, will not necessarily
                              make crossings safer. Marked crosswalks at signalized
                              intersections should be determined by an engineering assessment.
 Design Guidance and Considerations
• Marked crosswalks at signalized and unsignalized intersections should be determined by an
  engineering assessment.
• Because the effectiveness of marked crossings depends entirely on their visibility, maintaining
  marked crossings should be a high priority. Thermoplastic markings offer more durability than
  conventional paint.
• Consider consolidating arterial mid-block crosswalks and make them safer through various efforts.
 Planning Cost Estimates
    • $770 each. Crosswalks using other materials such as brick range from $2,500 to $5,000
        each.



                                                                                        Final Report | B-2
 High-Visibility Crosswalk
                                    High-visibility crosswalks may include a variety of crosswalk
                                    striping designs, such as ladder, continental, or bar pairs. A
                                    high-visibility crosswalk is much easier for an approaching
                                    motorist to see than traditional parallel lines. Research has
                                    found that high-visibility markings were detected at about twice
                                    the distance upstream as the transverse markings during
                                    daytime conditions.

 Design Guidance and Considerations
      • The high-visibility markings may be supplemented with the pedestrian crossing warning
        signs (sign W11-2 in the MUTCD) on each approach to the crosswalk.
    • Should be used at intersections with conflicts between vehicle and pedestrian movements,
        areas with high pedestrian traffic, and in commercial business districts.
    • An engineering study should be performed before a marked crosswalk is installed at a
        location away from a traffic control signal or an approach controlled by a STOP or YIELD
        sign.
 Planning Cost Estimates
    • $2,500 each



 Raised Crosswalk
                                       Raised crosswalks function as an extension of the sidewalk
                                       and allow pedestrians to have a greater prominence as they
                                       cross the street. Raised crossings make the pedestrian more
                                       prominent in the driver’s field of vision. Additionally,
                                       approach ramps may reduce vehicle speeds and improve
                                       motorist yielding.


 Design Guidance and Considerations
      • Typically installed on 2-lane or 3-lane roads with speed limits of 30 mph or less and annual
        average daily traffic (AADT) below about 9,000.
    • The crosswalk table is typically at least 10 FT wide and designed to allow the front and rear
        wheels of a passenger vehicle to be on top of the table at the same time.
    • Raised crossings are generally avoided on truck routes, emergency routes, and arterial
        streets.
    • Use detectable warnings at the curb edges to alert vision-impaired pedestrians that they are
        entering the roadway.
 Planning Cost Estimates
    • $8,000 each. Costs will vary based on the width of the road, as well as drainage
        conditions and the type of material used.




B-3 | Final Report
Median Refuge Island
                                 Median refuge islands are located at the mid-point of a
                                 marked crossing and help improve path user safety by
                                 allowing pedestrians to cross one direction of traffic at a time.
                                 Refuge islands minimize pedestrian exposure by shortening
                                 crossing distance and increasing the number of available
                                 gaps for crossing.
Design Guidance and Considerations
   •   May be appropriate on multi-lane roadways depending on speeds and volumes. Consider
       configuration with active warning beacons for improved yielding compliance.
   • Appropriate at signalized or unsignalized crosswalks.
   • Cut-through median refuge islands are preferred over curb ramps to better accommodate
       wheelchair users.
   • Requires 6 FT width between travel lanes (8 -10 FT preferred to accommodate bikes with
       trailers and wheelchair users). Clear width of 4 FT required, but preferably same width as
       crosswalk.
   • Refuge islands may collect road debris and may require somewhat frequent maintenance.
   • If a refuge island is landscaped, the landscaping should not compromise the visibility of
       pedestrians crossing in the crosswalk.
Planning Cost Estimates
   • $6,000 to $10,000 each. The cost for installing a raised concrete refuge island with
       landscaping ranges from $10,000 to $30,000.

Offset Crossings
                                         Offset crossings share similar characteristics with standard
                                         median refuge islands with one major difference – a
                                         staggered crossing. This offset is done to help orient the
                                         pedestrian toward oncoming traffic on the approach to
                                         each leg of the crossing. Eye contact between the
                                         pedestrian and motorist is improved at an offset crossing,
                                         which improves communication between the two
                                         roadway users.
Design Guidance and Considerations
   •   Offset crossings can be used in place of a standard median refuge island at most locations
       where the latter is appropriate; however, offset median refuge islands are typically installed
       at mid-block locations where there is an existing center turn lane or median at least 6 FT
       wide.
   • Offset crossings include refuge islands which may collect road debris and may require
       somewhat frequent maintenance. Trees and plantings must be maintained so as not to
       impair visibility.
Planning Cost Estimates
       Costs vary widely depending on the length and type, ranging from $10,000 to $40,000.




                                                                                       Final Report | B-4
 Rectangular Rapid Flash Beacon (RRFB)
                                 RRFBs are user-actuated amber LEDs that supplement warning
                                 signs at unsignalized intersections or midblock crosswalks. They
                                 can be activated by pedestrians manually by a push button or
                                 passively by a pedestrian detection system. The device includes
                                 two rectangular-shaped yellow indications, each with an LED-
                                 array-based light source, that flash with high frequency when
                                 activated. RRFBs have been shown to increase motor vehicle
                                 yielding compliance at crossings of multi-lane or high-volume
                                 roadways.
 Design Guidance and Considerations
      •   Use RRFBs at high volume pedestrian crossings or at priority bicycle route crossings,
          including shared use paths. RRFBs should not be installed in locations with sight distance
          constraints that limit the driver’s ability to view pedestrians as they approach the crosswalk.
      •   RRFBs are installed on both sides of the roadway at the edge of the crosswalk. If there is a
          pedestrian refuge or other median, an additional beacon should be installed in the median.
      •   Push buttons should be easy to identify and located on the right-hand side of the path. They
          should be positioned so that bicyclists do not have to dismount to activate.
      •   Depending on power supply, site conditions, the equipment used, and other variables,
          maintenance can be minimal. Solar powered RRFBs can help reduce costs.
 Planning Cost Estimates
    • $25,000 each

 High-intensity Activated CrossWalK (HAWK)
                                 A High-intensity Activated crossWalK (HAWK) functions
                                 similarly to a full traffic signal and offers the highest degree of
                                 motorist stopping through the use of a red signal indication.
                                 HAWKs are used to improve non-motorized crossings of major
                                 streets in locations where side street volumes do not support
                                 installation of a conventional traffic signal or where there are
                                 concerns that a conventional signal will encourage additional
                                 motor vehicle traffic on the minor street. HAWKs may also be
                                 used at midblock crossing locations.
 Design Guidance and Considerations
      • Suitable for arterial streets where speeds are 30-45 mph and there are three or more lanes
        of traffic in each direction (or two lanes with a median refuge).
    • Push buttons should be easy to identify and located on the right-hand side of the path. They
        should be positioned so that bicyclists do not have to dismount to activate.
    • Each crossing, regardless of traffic speed or volume, requires additional review by a
        registered engineer to identify sight lines, potential impacts on traffic progression, timing
        with adjacent signals, capacity and safety.
 Planning Cost Estimates
    • $300,000 each




B-5 | Final Report
Bicycle Facility Types
Selecting the best bikeway facility type for a given roadway can be challenging,
due to the range of factors that influence bicycle users’ comfort and safety. There
are no ‘hard and fast’ rules for determining the most appropriate type of facility
for a particular location – roadway speeds, volumes, right-of-way width, presence
of parking, adjacent land uses, and expected user types are all critical elements of
this decision. User preference varies with bicyclist’s skill level, trip purpose, and
individual characteristics, and no simple rule exists for determining what users
prefer. However, as the level of separation increases, a facility becomes more
attractive to a wider range of bicycle users—making bicycling a more viable and
preferred transportation mode.
Studies find that the most significant factors influencing bicycle use are motor
vehicle traffic volumes and speeds. Additionally, most bicyclists prefer facilities
separated from motor vehicle traffic or located on local roads with low motor
vehicle traffic speeds and volumes. Because off-street pathways are physically
separated from the roadway, they are perceived as safe and attractive routes for
bicyclists who prefer to avoid motor vehicle traffic.
Figure 3.1 presents contextual guidance for determining what type of bicycle
facility is best provided on a particular roadway based on speeds and volumes.
Furthermore, consistent use of treatments and application of bikeway facilities
allow users to anticipate whether they would feel comfortable riding on a
particular facility and plan their trips accordingly.


Figure 3.1. Bicycle Facility Guidance
                                                                     Average Annual Daily Traffic (1000 Veh/Day)
Facility Type         Street Class   0         2                4    6         8            10          15       20       25        30+
                       Local

                                                                                                                      Final Report | B-6
Bicycle Facility Classifications
This section summarizes the active transportation classification system and the associated facility
types.

 Signed Bike Route
 Signed Bike Routes are low-volume streets where motorists and bicyclists share the same space.
 Traffic calming and other treatments along the corridor reduce vehicle speeds so that motorists
 and bicyclists generally travel at the same speed, creating a more comfortable environment
 for all users.




 Design Guidance and Considerations

      •   Bike Route signage (MUTCD D11-1) should be applied at intervals frequent enough to
          keep bicyclists informed of changes in route direction and to remind motorists of the
          presence of bicyclists. Signs are commonly placed:
              o At the beginning and end of the bike route
              o At major changes in direction or at intersections with other bike routes
              o At intervals not to exceed ½ mile
      •   The AASHTO Guide for the Development of Bicycle Facilities recommends wide curb lanes
          with widths between 12 FT and 13.5 FT. Beyond this width, bike lanes may be provided.
      •   Bike route signage (MUTCD D11-1) costs $360 per installation.




B-7 | Final Report
Shared Lanes
Marked shared roadways use shared lane markings, also known as “sharrows,” to designate a
shared travel lane. Shared lane markings are used to encourage bicycle travel and remind
motorists of the potential presence of bicyclists within a narrow travel lane.




Design Guidance and Considerations
   •   Shared Lane Marking (SLM) stencils can serve a number of purposes, such as making
       motorists aware of the need to share the road with bicyclists, showing bicyclists the proper
       direction of travel, and, with correct placement, reminding bicyclists to keep a safe distance
       from parked cars to prevent “dooring” collisions.
   •   Place shared lane markings in the center of the effective travel lane to reduce marking wear
       and encourage cyclists to occupy the lane outside of the door zone of parked cars.
   •   Do not use on roads with speed limits higher than 35 mph.
   •   Markings should be placed immediately after intersections and spaced at 250 FT intervals
       thereafter.
   •   SLMs pair well with MUTCD R4-11 “Bikes May Use Full Lane” signs.
   •   SLMs cost $100 per location.




                                                                                      Final Report | B-8
 Bike Lane
 A Bike Lane is defined as a portion of the roadway that has been designated by striping, signage,
 and pavement markings for the preferential or exclusive use of bicyclists.




 Design Guidance and Considerations
      •   Bike lanes are most appropriate on arterial and collector streets where higher traffic
          volumes and speeds warrant greater separation. Bike lanes may also increase a rider’s
          comfort level, discourage bicyclists from riding on the sidewalk, and remind motorists that
          bicyclists have a right to the road.
      •   The desirable bike lane width adjacent to a curb face is 6 FT. The desirable ridable surface
          adjacent to a street edge or longitudinal joint is minimum of 4 FT. In areas where illegal
          parking in bike lanes is a concern, 5 FT wide bike lanes may be preferred.
      •   Wider bicycle lanes are desirable in certain situations such as on higher speed arterials (45
          mph+) where use of a wider bicycle lane would increase separation between passing
          vehicles and bicyclists. Appropriate signing and stenciling is important with wide bicycle
          lanes to ensure motorists do not mistake the lane for a vehicle lane or parking lane.
      •   A bike lane next to a parking lane shall be at least 5 FT wide, unless there is a marked
          buffer between them. Wherever possible, minimize parking lane width in favor of increased
          bike lane width.
      •   Bike lane striping costs: $12,000 per mile
              o 10% contingency
              o 20% design
              o Does not include additional pavement, stencils, signage




B-9 | Final Report
Buffered Bike Lane
Buffered bike lanes are conventional bicycle lanes paired with a designated buffer space,
separating the bicycle lane from the adjacent motor vehicle travel lane and/or parking lane.




Design Guidance and Considerations
   •   This treatment is appropriate for bike lanes on roadways with high motor vehicle traffic
       volumes and speed, adjacent to parking lanes, or a high volume of truck or oversized
       vehicle traffic.
   •   Buffers should be at least 2 FT wide. If 3 FT or wider, mark with diagonal or chevron
       hatching. For clarity at driveways or minor street crossings, consider a dotted line for the
       inside buffer boundary where cars are expected to cross.
   •   Where bicyclist volumes are high, bicyclist speed differentials are significant, or where side-
       by-side riding is desired, the desired bicycle travel area width is 7 FT.
   •   Parking side buffers are helpful in areas with high turnover parking to reduce the risk of
       dooring.
   •   Bicycle lane wording and/or symbol and arrow markings (MUTCD 9C-3) should be used to
       define the bike lane and designate that portion of the street for preferential use by bicyclists.
   •   Color may be used at the beginning of each block to discourage motorists from entering
       the buffered lane.
   •   Buffered bike lane striping costs: $16,000 per mile
           o 10% contingency
           o 20% design
           o Does not include additional pavement, stencils, signage




                                                                                        Final Report | B-10
 Protected Bike Lane
 By separating bicyclists from motor traffic, “protected” or physically separated bike lanes can offer
 a higher level of comfort than conventional bike lanes. The separating element can include flexible
 delineators, extruded curbs, on-street parking, or other barriers.




 Design Guidance and Considerations
      •   Protected bike lanes can be accomplished by combining a painted buffer with a physical
          barrier such as flexible bollards, a landscaped buffer, or a parking lane.
      •   Dedicated and protected space for bicyclists makes a facility attractive for riders of all levels
          and ages.
      •   The minimum desired width should be 5 FT in constrained locations. In areas with high
          bicyclist volumes or uphill sections, the minimum desired width should be 7 FT to allow for
          bicyclists passing each other.
      •   Separation width depends on physical separation method. Minimum separation width next
          to parking is 3 FT to accommodate opening doors.
      •   Pavement markings, symbols, and/or arrow markings must be placed at the beginning of
          the separated bike lane and at intervals along the facility based on engineering judgment to
          define the bike direction.
      •   Protected bike lanes should be maintained in order to be free of potholes, broken glass,
          and other debris.
      •   Costs will ultimately include the striping both a bike lane, a buffer zone, and costs of the
          physical separator. Costs vary greatly depending on the type of separation provided.
          Flexible bollards typically range from $100 to $600 per pole.




B-11 | Final Report
Paved Shoulder
Typically found in less dense or unincorporated areas, paved shoulders are paved, striped
shoulders wide enough for bicycle travel.




Design Guidance and Considerations
   •   Appropriate on roads with moderate to high volumes and speeds and on roadways with a
       large amount of truck traffic.
   •   Paved shoulders are generally used by commuter and long-distance recreational riders,
       rather than families with children or more inexperienced riders.
   •   Shoulder width of at least 5 FT recommended, but additional space should be provided on
       roads if there are higher levels of bicycle usage, if motor vehicle speeds are high or if there
       is a higher percentage of truck and bus traffic.
   •   If the shoulder has rumble strips designed to alert swerving motorists, there should still be at
       least four feet of "rideable" surface for bicyclists. Periodic gaps in the rumble strips should
       also be provided to allow bicyclists to travel across the rumble strip as needed.
   •   Where paved shoulders are present, accommodations should be made for bicyclists
       through the intersection.
   •   Costs: $30,000 per mile
